Health officials confirm 4 mpox cases in Stanislaus County - FOX40

News Brief
Health officials in Stanislaus County, California, confirmed four cases of mpox on October 30, 2026. Two of the cases were identified as Clade I, while the other two are still under testing. Three of the individuals involved in the cases reported no international travel, and the travel history of the fourth is being assessed. Officials noted that the risk to the general public remains low, but they urged residents to be aware of symptoms and take preventive measures. The Stanislaus County Health Services Agency is working with the California Department of Public Health to monitor the situation and prevent further spread. Prevention methods include the JYNNEOS vaccine for those at higher risk and avoiding close contact with anyone showing signs of mpox. The cases highlight a trend of mpox spreading among people without recent international travel, which has been observed since August 2026.
Original source text (English)
FOX40 News Health officials confirm 4 mpox cases in Stanislaus County Natalie Sierra 2 days ago (FOX40.COM) — Officials with the Stanislaus County Public Health are reporting four confirmed cases of mpox, also known as monkeypox, in the area. The California Department of Public Health confirmed that two of the cases are Clade I, while the other two are still being tested. Three of the people reported that they did not travel internationally, and the travel history for the fourth person is still being assessed. “Historically, Clade I mpox cases in the U.S. were linked to international travel. However, since August 2026, more cases have been reported among people without recent international travel,” health officials said. According to officials, the recently confirmed cases of mpox having no history of international travel suggest that the virus may now be spreading within U.S. communities. The Stanislaus County Health Services Agency is working with CDPH to monitor mpox activity and prevent further spread. Officials assure community members that the risk of contracting the infection remains low, however, they should be familiar with the symptoms as a precaution. “While we have identified four cases in our county, we want residents to know that the risk to the general public remains low,” Dr. Thea Papasozomenos, Stanislaus County Public Health officer, said. “Being aware of the symptoms, getting vaccinated if at risk of exposure to mpox, and seeking medical care when needed are important ways to protect yourself and prevent the spread of mpox.” Mpox, a viral infection, causes painful or itchy rashes and flu-like symptoms: A rash or sores that may look like pimples or blisters Fever, chills, or swollen lymph nodes Headache, muscle aches, or fatigue The two main types are Clade I and Clade II, which both spread through close, personal contact with someone infected. This includes skin-to-skin contact, intimate or sexual contact and contact with infected sores or bodily fluids. “It can also spread through shared items such as bedding, towels, or clothing that have been contaminated with the virus,” officials said. Prevention methods include the JYNNEOS vaccine for people at increased risk of mpox exposure and avoiding close contact with anyone who has an unexplained rash or sores. More information on mpox can be found here. More information on vaccines can be found here. Ad Your Privacy Choices

B routine priorityPublic-health news

Pakistan: Four New Mpox Cases Reported In Khyber Pakhtunkhwa - Ommcom News

News Brief
On October 28, 2026, four new mpox cases were reported in Khyber Pakhtunkhwa province, Pakistan, bringing the total infections in the province to 18. Peshawar, the provincial capital, reported the highest number of cases, with five infections, and one death was recorded. The provincial health department confirmed these cases, and a case in Karachi, Sindh province, was previously reported last month. Mpox spreads through close contact, bodily fluids, respiratory particles, and contaminated objects, posing a risk to communities and health workers. Authorities are conducting surveillance and monitoring efforts to prevent further spread, and the department advised avoiding close contact with infected individuals. Prevention relies on awareness and education, with WHO recommending vaccines for those most at risk, including health workers and individuals with close contact with infected persons.

Feedback RSS Feed Sitemap Home Odisha Nation World Sports Business Entertainment Videos Science & Tech Photo Gallery Odisha Special No Result View All Result Home Odisha Nation World Sports Business Entertainment Videos Science & Tech Photo Gallery Odisha Special No Result View All Result Home World Pakistan: Four New Mpox Cases Reported In Khyber Pakhtunkhwa by OMMCOM NEWS October 28, 2026 in World Islamabad: Four new cases of mpox have been reported in Pakistan’s northwest Khyber Pakhtunkhwa province, bringing the total number of infections in the province to 18, the provincial health department said on Tuesday. Peshawar, the provincial capital, has reported the highest number of cases, with five infections, while one death from the disease has been recorded so far, the department said, Xinhua News Agency reported. Symptoms of mpox include fever, headache, body aches and skin rashes or blisters. The department advised people to avoid close contact with infected individuals to reduce the risk of transmission. Last month, a case of monkeypox was confirmed in Pakistan’s southern port city of Karachi in Sindh province, the provincial health department said. According to hospital officials, a 45-year-old resident of Karachi’s Surjani town tested positive for the virus through a PCR test after developing a rash. The patient was brought to the Sindh Infectious Diseases Hospital and was undergoing treatment at the facility, officials had said. The latest case increased the number of confirmed monkeypox infections in Sindh province to 42 this year, including 14 cases reported in Karachi, according to the provincial health department. Authorities were conducting surveillance and monitoring efforts to prevent further spread of the disease. Mpox, previously known as monkeypox, is a viral disease caused by an orthopoxvirus called monkeypox virus and spreads primarily through close contact with someone who has mpox, causing a painful rash, enlarged lymph nodes and fever. It can make people very sick and leave scars. The disease mainly spreads from person to person through close contact, according to the World Health Organization (WHO) statement. Transmission occurs through exposure to bodily fluids; lesions on the skin or on internal mucosal surfaces, such as in the mouth or throat; respiratory particles; and contaminated objects. Prevention and control of mpox rely on raising awareness in communities and educating health workers to prevent infection and stop transmission. WHO recommends the use of vaccines for those most at risk of exposure during outbreaks, including people who have had close contact with someone with mpox, and health workers. People who were vaccinated against smallpox in childhood may have some cross-protection against mpox. Pakistan: Four New Mpox Cases Surface in Khyber Pakhtunkhwa, Total Reaches 18 - Lokmat Times

The provincial health department confirmed the new cases, highlighting an ongoing public health concern. Peshawar, the provincial capital, has recorded the highest number of cases, with five infections. Tragically, one death related to mpox has also been reported in the province. Symptoms associated with mpox include fever, headache, body aches, and characteristic skin rashes or blisters. Health officials are advising residents to maintain distance from infected individuals to curb further transmission. Separately, in Pakistan's southern port city of Karachi, Sindh province, authorities have confirmed 42 mpox infections this year, with 14 cases originating from Karachi itself. Health departments across Pakistan are actively monitoring the situation and implementing surveillance measures to prevent a wider spread of the disease. Mpox, previously known as monkeypox, is a viral illness caused by the monkeypox virus. It primarily spreads through close contact, leading to painful rashes, enlarged lymph nodes, and fever. The World Health Organization (WHO) emphasizes community awareness and health worker education as key prevention strategies. 独立小组的新报告 - 显而易见的隐患:猴痘 与忽视已知威胁的成本

A New Report From The Independent Panel - Hiding In Plain Sight: Mpox And The Cost Of Neglecting Known Threats
新闻简报独立小组发布的报告《显而易见的隐患:猴痘 与忽视已知威胁的成本》于 2026 年 10 月 31 日发表,强调了 猴痘 的全球传播,该病毒现已成为全球公共卫生风险。报告指出,猴痘 曾一度是西非和中非地区的偶发性疾病,但到 2026 年已导致 82 个国家出现 暴发,其中马达加斯加面临 2026 年最大的 暴发,截至 2026 年 8 月中旬,已报告超过 3,400 例病例和 22 例死亡。报告还指出,猴痘病毒的新变种已出现,增加了对人群的风险并给卫生系统带来压力。它批评了在疫区未能优先控制 猴痘 的情况,特别是在刚果民主共和国,卫生工作者需应对包括埃博拉在内的多种挑战。报告强调,某些国家在 2024–2025 年的 暴发 中部署了 疫苗,但对其影响仍存在不确定性。2026 年 8 月宣布了 疫苗 的储备,但快速诊断测试和治疗手段并未在最需要的地方提供。报告呼吁持续投资社区系统,以预防未来的公共卫生紧急情况,并更好地应对下一次大流行威胁。
查看新闻五要素
时间
该报告于 2026 年 10 月 31 日星期六发布,讨论了 2026 年 猴痘 的全球传播,特别提到了截至 2026 年 8 月中旬马达加斯加的 暴发 情况。
地点与对象
该报告涵盖了多个国家的 猴痘 暴发情况,包括刚果民主共和国和马达加斯加,截至 2026 年,该病毒已在全球 82 个国家传播。
事件
独立小组的报告强调了 猴痘 的全球传播、新变种的出现以及在疫区未能优先采取控制措施,导致可预防的公共卫生紧急事件。
规模、影响与风险
截至 2026 年 8 月中旬,马达加斯加报告了超过 3,400 例病例和 22 例死亡。在欧盟/欧洲经济区,过去 12 个月内报告了近 3,800 例确认的 猴痘 病例,且有本地 传播 的迹象。报告强调了对卫生系统造成的负担以及 猴痘 对易感人群的风险。
应对、状态与不确定性
报告指出,某些国家在 2024–2025 年的 暴发 中部署了 疫苗,但表示其影响尚不明确。2026 年 8 月宣布了 疫苗 的储备,但快速诊断测试和治疗手段并未在最需要的地方提供。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

A New Report From The Independent Panel - Hiding In Plain Sight: Mpox And The Cost Of Neglecting Known Threats

News Brief
The Independent Panel's report, 'Hiding in Plain Sight: Mpox and the Cost of Neglecting Known Threats,' published on 31 October 2026, highlights the global spread of mpox, which has now become a widespread public health risk. The report states that mpox, once a sporadic disease in West and Central Africa, has caused outbreaks in 82 countries by 2026, with Madagascar facing the largest outbreak of 2026, reporting over 3,400 cases and 22 deaths as of mid-August. The report also notes that new variants of the monkeypox virus have emerged, increasing risks for people and straining health systems. It criticizes the failure to prioritize mpox control in endemic regions, particularly the Democratic Republic of the Congo, where health workers manage multiple challenges, including Ebola. The report emphasizes that vaccines were deployed in some countries during the 2024–2025 outbreaks but remains uncertain about their impact. A vaccine stockpile was announced in August 2026, but rapid diagnostic tests and therapies were not available where they were most needed. The report calls for sustained investment in community systems to prevent future health emergencies and better prepare for the next pandemic threat.
Original source text (English)
Scoop has an Ethical Paywall Licence needed for work use Start Free Trial  World  Contact  About  ScoopPro  Scoop Werewolf Wellington The Dig Business Scoop Pacific Community Review of Books InfoPages Front Page Scoops Parliament Politics Regional Business Sci-Tech World Culture Education Health    Contact  About  ScoopPro Scoop Front Page Scoops Parliament Politics Regional Business Sci-Tech World Culture Education Health Network Scoop Werewolf Wellington Scoop The Dig Business Scoop Pacific Community Review of Books InfoPages World Video | Defence | Foreign Affairs | Natural Events | Trade | NZ in World News | NZ National News Video | NZ Regional News | Search Scoop  World   A New Report From The Independent Panel - Hiding In Plain Sight: Mpox And The Cost Of Neglecting Known Threats Saturday, 31 October 2026, 3:34 pm Press Release: The Independent Panel A new report from The Independent Panel for Pandemic Preparedness and Response traces how mpox, once a sporadic disease in West and Central Africa mainly caused by contact with infected animals, is now causing outbreaks in dozens of countries around the world and counts humans as a reservoir. The Independent Panel’s latest report – Hiding in Plain Sight: Mpox and the Cost of Neglecting Known Threats – finds that the long-term neglect of mpox contributed to two preventable global health emergencies and made the disease a widespread public health risk. The report also shows how new variants of the monkeypox virus have emerged over a relatively short period of time, adding risks to people and burdens on already stretched health systems. “We must stop ignoring sporadic diseases with epidemic potential,” said the Right Honourable Helen Clark, Co-Chair of The Independent Panel. “Mpox was neglected at our peril. Preventable health emergencies are a sign of failure. They happen when community health care is neglected, signals are missed, and pathogens are allowed to spread beyond their original habitats. These gaps leave us all vulnerable.” Mpox is nowhere near as dangerous as smallpox, caused by the related variola virus, but it causes painful lesions that last weeks, may require hospitalisation, is more dangerous for people with advanced or untreated HIV, and has caused foetal and child deaths. “There were earlier opportunities to control mpox, which was primarily a zoonotic illness in endemic countries in West and Central Africa,” said the report’s lead author, Dr Els Torreele. Advertisement - scroll to continue reading “With 145 countries reporting mpox in the last four years and many outbreaks of sustained human-to-human transmission, the opportunity to stop it locally was missed. We are all paying a price with a disease that now counts humans as a reservoir.” The right tools were not in the right place at the right time The report documents that prior to 2022, smallpox vaccines and treatments with potential against mpox existed because of health security investments and biodefence research. However, these were first used widely to support mpox control during the 2022 global emergency, when high-income countries were also affected. This was an opportunity to document the vaccine’s efficacy against mpox, but it was still not made available in the original endemic regions – particularly in the Democratic Republic of the Congo, which had been reporting around 2,000 suspected cases each year. Vaccines against mpox were deployed on the African continent only after the emergence of a new viral subtype, a cross-border surge and the declaration of a second public health emergency of international concern in August 2024. When finally available, vaccines were provided to several countries experiencing outbreaks in a staggered manner. Ultimately, it is unclear how much impact they had on the 2024–2025 mpox outbreaks. An mpox vaccine stockpile was announced only in August 2026. Further, tools like rapid diagnostic tests and therapies were not available where they were most needed. Only about one-third of suspected cases in the Democratic Republic of the Congo could be confirmed with testing “Many of the lessons of mpox are similar to those from the COVID-19 experience,” said Dr Joanne Liu, a member of The Independent Panel. “Tools like vaccines were not prioritised for the people who needed them most. And the one tool that would have made the biggest difference – an affordable, rapid diagnostic test that could be used in communities – still does not exist.” Lessons must be applied and not only learned Lessons from mpox are being relearned in 2026, Independent Panel Co-Chairs Helen Clark and Ellen Johnson Sirleaf write in the report’s foreword. “The Ebola Bundibugyo virus has caused two prior outbreaks in this century. Yet it too surprised the world in 2026, and communities affected were stranded with no near point-of-care tests, no vaccines, no treatments, and eroded trust in government and institutions. A world that cannot manage known diseases will not be ready for Disease X, the next pandemic threat.” Mpox is now global The consequences are still unfolding. In 2026, 82 countries have confirmed more than 14,000 cases. Madagascar, which had never previously recorded an mpox case, now faces the world’s largest outbreak of 2026 with more than 3,400 cases and 22 deaths as of mid-August. In the EU/EEA in the last 12 months, almost 3,800 confirmed mpox cases were reported, and among people with known travel history, most had not travelled, indicating local transmission. People continue to be affected in the Democratic Republic of the Congo, where health workers must manage multiple health challenges, including Ebola. Focus on community systems to avoid emergencies "Instead of parachuting in emergency funds for community engagement, leaving behind posters that fade on clinic walls, communities must be supported through sustained investment that builds lasting resilience.” said Dr Torreele. “A response should not only be evaluated through numbers of cases, countries affected, or doses of vaccines deployed, but also by how it leaves communities at risk better prepared and able to stay healthy.” The report is the result of a review of literature and in-depth conversations with people directly involved in the two mpox emergencies and in ongoing mpox research. The lessons explained in the report are considered in the context of the main recommendations of The Independent Panel to address gaps in pandemic prevention, preparedness, and response as set out in its 2021 report, COVID-19: Make it the Last Pandemic. © Scoop Media Using Scoop for work? Chickenpox Virus Dominated Rash Illnesses Mistaken for Mpox in Sierra Leone - Bioengineer.org
新闻简报During Sierra Leone’s 2025 mpox epidemic, a study found that over half of the patients who met the clinical case definition for mpox but tested negative for the mpox virus were infected with other rash-causing pathogens. Varicella-zoster virus, the agent of chickenpox, was the most frequently detected, accounting for 43.3 percent of cases. The study, conducted between June 16 and August 27, 2025, used two PCR panels to test 407 suspected mpox cases, with 263 being MPXV-negative. These samples were collected from eight health districts, including Port Loko and Koinadugu, and showed a detection rate of 58.2 percent. The authors emphasized that while varicella-zoster virus was present in all districts with positive cases, no epidemiological link could be established between the co-detected pathogens and the subsequent Haemophilus ducreyi outbreak. The study also noted limitations, including incomplete clinical data and the absence of sequencing, which prevented a full assessment of the clinical impact of co-detections.
查看新闻五要素
时间
该研究于2025年6月16日至8月27日进行,发表日期为2026年11月2日。Haemophilus ducreyi相关暴发的暴发于2025年12月正式宣布。
地点与对象
The study took place in Sierra Leone, specifically in eight health districts, including Port Loko, Koinadugu, and Tonkolili. The population included patients of all ages, with a median age of 18 years and 44.5 percent aged 15 or younger.
事件
在塞拉利昂2025年猴痘疫情期间,一项回顾性实验室基于的监测研究发现,超过一半的符合猴痘临床病例定义但未检测到猴痘病毒的患者携带其他引起皮疹的病原体,其中水痘-带状疱疹病毒是最常检测到的。
规模、影响与风险
Out of 407 suspected mpox cases, 144 were positive for MPXV and excluded from the differential analysis. The remaining 263 MPXV-negative samples showed a detection rate of 58.2 percent, with varicella-zoster virus detected in 43.3 percent of cases. No explicit risk level was stated, but the authors highlighted the potential for real-time differential testing to provide early warning signals.
应对、状态与不确定性
该研究是国家疫情应对的一部分。作者警告称,由于样本数量较少,无法对共检病原体之间的生物相互作用进行推断。研究结果仅代表一个时间点的快照,不能建立这三种病原体与随后Haemophilus ducreyi相关暴发之间的流行病学联系。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Chickenpox Virus Dominated Rash Illnesses Mistaken for Mpox in Sierra Leone - Bioengineer.org

News Brief
During Sierra Leone’s 2025 mpox epidemic, a study found that over half of the patients who met the clinical case definition for mpox but tested negative for the mpox virus were infected with other rash-causing pathogens. Varicella-zoster virus, the agent of chickenpox, was the most frequently detected, accounting for 43.3 percent of cases. The study, conducted between June 16 and August 27, 2025, used two PCR panels to test 407 suspected mpox cases, with 263 being MPXV-negative. These samples were collected from eight health districts, including Port Loko and Koinadugu, and showed a detection rate of 58.2 percent. The authors emphasized that while varicella-zoster virus was present in all districts with positive cases, no epidemiological link could be established between the co-detected pathogens and the subsequent Haemophilus ducreyi outbreak. The study also noted limitations, including incomplete clinical data and the absence of sequencing, which prevented a full assessment of the clinical impact of co-detections.
Original source text (English)
HOME NEWS EXPLORE CAREER Companies Jobs EVENTS iGEM News Team PHOTOS VIDEO WIKI BLOG COMMUNITY FACEBOOK INSTAGRAM TWITTER Monday, November 2, 2026 No Result View All Result Login HOME NEWS EXPLORE CAREER Companies Jobs Lecturer PhD Studentship Postdoc Research Assistant EVENTS iGEM News Team PHOTOS VIDEO WIKI BLOG COMMUNITY FACEBOOK INSTAGRAM TWITTER HOME NEWS EXPLORE CAREER Companies Jobs Lecturer PhD Studentship Postdoc Research Assistant EVENTS iGEM News Team PHOTOS VIDEO WIKI BLOG COMMUNITY FACEBOOK INSTAGRAM TWITTER No Result View All Result No Result View All Result Home NEWS Science News Health Chickenpox Virus Dominated Rash Illnesses Mistaken for Mpox in Sierra Leone by October 29, 2026 in Health Reading Time: 4 mins read 0 Chickenpox Virus Dominated Rash Illnesses Mistaken for Mpox in Sierra Leone Share on FacebookShare on TwitterShare on LinkedinShare on RedditShare on Telegram During Sierra Leone’s 2025 mpox epidemic, more than half of the patients who met the clinical case definition for mpox but tested negative for the mpox virus were carrying other rash-causing pathogens, with varicella-zoster virus — the agent of chickenpox and shingles — accounting for the vast majority of those detections. That is the central finding of a retrospective laboratory-based surveillance study published in New Microbes and New Infections, which screened samples from MPXV-negative suspected mpox cases using two commercial multiplex PCR panels across eight health districts. The study was conducted between June 16 and August 27, 2025, as part of the national epidemic response. Two mobile laboratories, deployed at the regional hospital in Port Loko and at the Koinadugu District Government Hospital, processed clinical specimens — predominantly lesion swabs, along with a small number of vaginal swabs and crust samples — from patients meeting the World Health Organization mpox case definition. During active case searching, the definition was broadened to include anyone presenting with a severe rash, even if other criteria were not met. In total, 407 suspected mpox cases were sampled and tested for MPXV by quantitative PCR during the study window. Of these, 144 were positive, corresponding to a positivity rate of 35.7 percent, and were excluded from the differential analysis. The remaining 263 MPXV-negative samples — 252 lesion swabs, seven vaginal swabs, and four crust samples — all passed an endogenous control quality check and were retained for further testing. Discover more Study Biology Check Symptoms Read Med Journals Find Skin Care Buy Maps The researchers used the Allplex Meningitis-V1 Assay, previously validated on cutaneous samples with next-generation sequencing, to detect seven herpesviruses: herpes simplex virus types 1 and 2, varicella-zoster virus, Epstein-Barr virus, cytomegalovirus, and human herpesviruses 6 and 7. A second panel, the Allplex Genital Ulcer Assay, targeted the bacteria Haemophilus ducreyi, Treponema pallidum, and Chlamydia trachomatis, the causative agent of lymphogranuloma venereum, alongside overlapping viral targets. A sample was considered positive if a target was detected with a cycle threshold value below 35, and weak or late amplification signals were re-tested in duplicate. For the four targets covered by both panels, duplicate testing yielded 100 percent concordance. The results showed that at least one target was identified in 153 of the 263 samples, an overall detection rate of 58.2 percent. Varicella-zoster virus was by far the most frequently detected pathogen, found in 43.3 percent of patients (114 of 263; 95 percent confidence interval 37.3 to 49.6). Among the other herpesviruses, HSV-2 showed the highest detection frequency at 7.2 percent, followed by cytomegalovirus at 6.85 percent and Epstein-Barr virus at 6.1 percent. Lower rates were recorded for HHV-7 (4.6 percent), HSV-1 (1.9 percent), and HHV-6 (0.4 percent). Among bacterial targets, Haemophilus ducreyi was detected in 1.1 percent of cases, while every sample tested negative for Treponema pallidum and Chlamydia trachomatis. The study population was young: ages ranged from 1 to 80 years with a median of 18 years, and 44.5 percent of participants were aged 15 or younger. Gender information was available for 259 participants, of whom 58.7 percent were male. Statistically significant associations with age emerged for Epstein-Barr virus and HSV-2, both detected at higher proportions in older age groups, with detection rising progressively across the three age strata. Cytomegalovirus and Epstein-Barr virus were the only pathogens showing significant sex-based differences, both more frequent among females. Varicella-zoster virus showed no significant demographic associations, although nearly 48 percent of children aged 15 or younger tested positive compared with 39 percent of patients over 30, a difference that did not reach statistical significance. Co-detections were common and often complex. Combinations involving varicella-zoster virus were the most frequent, occurring in 19 samples, most often alongside HSV-2 and cytomegalovirus. One sample was positive for five herpesviruses simultaneously — varicella-zoster virus, cytomegalovirus, HSV-2, HHV-7, and Epstein-Barr virus — and two samples carried triple viral infections. Haemophilus ducreyi was exclusively co-detected with varicella-zoster virus, on two occasions, a molecular co-detection the authors describe as rarely reported. Among co-detections not involving varicella-zoster virus, nine of ten included Epstein-Barr virus. The authors caution that, given the small numbers, no inference about biological interaction between co-detected pathogens can be made. Discover more Shop African Art Explore History Track Outbreaks Uninterruptible power supplies Applied science journals Geographically, the highest sample numbers came from Port Loko and Tonkolili, and detections of at least one target exceeded undiagnosed cases in five of the eight surveyed districts. Varicella-zoster virus was the only pathogen identified in every district where positive cases were found, with the largest counts in Koinadugu, Falaba, and Port Loko. The distribution of other pathogens varied: HSV-2 and cytomegalovirus clustered in Port Loko and Tonkolili, Epstein-Barr virus in Kambia, and HHV-7 in Kambia and Bombali. One observation carries particular public health weight. The retrospective screening identified Haemophilus ducreyi in three samples collected months before an outbreak of Haemophilus ducreyi-associated skin ulceration was officially declared in Sierra Leone for the first time in December 2025, with the reported likely index case showing symptom onset on October 1, 2025. The authors stress that their findings do not establish an epidemiological link between these three patients and the subsequent outbreak, but they argue that real-time differential testing could potentially have provided an early warning signal prompting further investigation. The study has notable limitations. Samples and epidemiological data were originally collected for the mpox response, leaving critical clinical information — including symptoms, HIV status, and patient outcomes — incomplete, so the clinical impact of co-detections could not be assessed. MPXV-positive samples were not included in the differential testing, sequencing was not performed, and the study period covered neither the entire outbreak timeline nor every district in Sierra Leone. The authors also note that pathogen distributions can vary seasonally, meaning the findings represent a temporal snapshot rather than a complete annual profile, which may partly explain the absence of Chlamydia trachomatis and Treponema pallidum detections. Even so, the results demonstrate the feasibility of integrating multiplex molecular screening into outbreak response, particularly when combined with mobile laboratory deployment, to reduce diagnostic dead ends and uncover pathogens circulating beneath the epidemiological signal of a dominant outbreak agent. Subject of Research: Retrospective multiplex PCR screening of MPXV-negative suspected mpox cases in Sierra Leone in 2025 to identify alternative viral and bacterial pathogens. B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

非性传播获得的猴痘与更高的住院率相关

Nonsexually Acquired Mpox Associated With Higher Hospitalization Rates
新闻简报一项在IDWeek 2026上展示的研究分析了2022年5月至2024年12月期间加利福尼亚州的猴痘病例,比较了非性传播和性传播的传播病例。研究人员发现,非性传播获得的II型猴痘与更高的住院率(12.5% vs 4.7%)、更高的HIV 患病率(53.3% vs 39.8%)以及比性传播病例更低的疫苗接种率相关。在6651例报告病例中,有561例(10.8%)被归类为非性传播获得,具有显著的人口统计学差异,包括更多出生时被指定为女性的个体以及年龄小于18岁的个体。该研究还强调了社会经济差异,非性传播病例更常出现在健康表现指数(HPI)得分较低的地区。研究人员得出结论,需要更多信息来了解非性传播猴痘 传播的动态,并制定针对性的预防策略。所有差异均基于不重叠的95%置信区间被认定为具有统计学意义。
查看新闻五要素
时间
该研究于2022年5月至2024年12月期间进行,于2026年10月27日发表。
地点与对象
该研究聚焦于加利福尼亚州的猴痘病例,比较了非性传播获得猴痘的患者与性传播获得猴痘的患者,包括人口统计学和临床特征。
事件
研究人员评估了加利福尼亚州性传播和非性传播获得的II型猴痘患者的人口统计学和临床差异,发现非性传播病例与更高的住院率、更高的HIV 患病率以及更低的疫苗接种率相关。
规模、影响与风险
在6651例报告的猴痘病例中,有561例(10.8%)被归类为非性传播获得,其中12.5%的患者住院,而性传播暴露组的住院率为4.7%。非性传播组的HIV 患病率为53.3%,而性传播组为39.8%。非性传播组的疫苗接种率也较低(96.8%未接种疫苗 vs 性传播组的87.9%)。
应对、状态与不确定性
该研究在IDWeek 2026上展示,所有报告的差异均基于不重叠的95%置信区间被认定为具有统计学意义。研究人员强调需要进一步研究非性传播猴痘 传播的动态,以制定针对性策略。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Nonsexually Acquired Mpox Associated With Higher Hospitalization Rates

News Brief
A study presented at IDWeek 2026 analyzed mpox cases in California between May 2022 and December 2024, comparing those with nonsexual and sexual transmission. Researchers found that nonsexually acquired clade II mpox was associated with higher hospitalization rates (12.5% vs 4.7%), a higher prevalence of HIV (53.3% vs 39.8%), and lower vaccination rates compared to sexually acquired cases. Of 6651 reported cases, 561 (10.8%) were classified as nonsexually acquired, with notable demographic differences, including a higher proportion of individuals assigned female at birth and younger than 18 years. The study also highlighted socioeconomic disparities, with nonsexual cases more frequently occurring in areas with lower Health Performance Index (HPI) scores. The researchers concluded that more information is needed to understand the dynamics of non-sexual mpox transmission and develop targeted prevention strategies. All differences were considered statistically significant based on nonoverlapping 95% confidence intervals.

Compared with sexual transmission, nonsexually acquired clade II mpox is associated with an increased risk for hospitalization, a higher prevalence of HIV, and lower vaccination rates. Patients with clade II mpox (previously monkeypox) resulting from nonsexual transmission are more frequently hospitalized and have a higher prevalence of HIV than those who report recent sexual contact, according to study results presented at IDWeek 2026, held from October 21 to 24, in Washington, DC. Researchers evaluated differences in demographic characteristics, clinical outcomes, and potential exposures among patients with sexually and nonsexually acquired mpox in California. The analysis included confirmed and probable clade II mpox cases reported to the California Department of Public Health between May 2022 and December 2024. Drawing on public health surveillance records, the researchers sorted cases into groups based on whether patients reported sexual activity in the 21 days before their symptoms began. Patients who denied sexual contact during this period were included in the nonsexual exposure group only if their initial lesions occurred outside the genital and perianal regions. Those reporting no sexual contact but presenting with lesions initially involving these areas were excluded from that group. More information is needed on the dynamics of which populations may be at risk for non-sexual mpox transmission for targeted messaging and strategies. Demographic and clinical characteristics were compared using proportions via the Wilson method. Statistical significance was assessed based on whether the CIs overlapped between groups. Of 6651 reported mpox cases, 5206 had sufficient information to determine sexual exposure classification. Among these patients, 561 (10.8%) were categorized as having nonsexually acquired mpox, while 4645 (89.2%) reported recent sexual contact. Hospitalization occurred in 12.5% of patients in the nonsexual exposure group compared with 4.7% in the sexual exposure group. The prevalence of HIV was also higher among patients classified as having nonsexually acquired mpox (53.3% vs 39.8%). The researchers reported differences in vaccination history and potential environmental exposure. Most patients in both groups were unvaccinated against mpox, although the proportion was higher among those in the nonsexual group (96.8% vs 87.9%). Receipt of 1 vaccine dose was reported in 2.3% vs 5.6% of patients, respectively, while 0.9% vs 6.5% had received 2 doses. Possible exposure through contaminated objects or surfaces was reported by 16.4% of patients in the nonsexual group compared with 10.0% of those in the sexual exposure group. Patients classified as having nonsexually acquired mpox also differed by age, sex at birth, and race and ethnicity. The proportion assigned female at birth was higher in the nonsexual group (4.6% vs 2.2%), as was the proportion younger than 18 years (2.3% vs 0.3%). Black patients accounted for 17.5% of nonsexual cases and 10.8% of sexual cases, while Hispanic or Latino patients represented 53.5% and 43.5%, respectively. White patients accounted for a smaller proportion of the nonsexual group than the sexual exposure group (18.5% vs 32.2%). Regarding sexual orientation, 20.0% of patients in the nonsexual group identified as heterosexual compared with 7.3% of those in the sexual exposure group. Conversely, 54.2% vs 73.7% identified as gay, lesbian, or same-gender loving. Patients declining to disclose their sexual orientation represented 5.9% and 5.4% of the respective groups. Geographic socioeconomic differences were identified using the Health Performance Index (HPI). Residence in an area within the lowest HPI quartile was more common among patients in the nonsexual group (38.5% vs 23.4%), whereas residence in the highest quartile was less common (14.5% vs 27.9%). All reported differences were considered statistically significant based on nonoverlapping 95% CIs. “More information is needed on the dynamics of which populations may be at risk for non-sexual mpox transmission for targeted messaging and strategies,” the researchers concluded. Event

Scale, impact and risk
Among 6651 reported mpox cases, 561 (10.8%) were categorized as nonsexually acquired, with 12.5% hospitalized compared to 4.7% in the sexual exposure group. HIV prevalence was 53.3% in the nonsexual group versus 39.8% in the sexual group. Vaccination rates were also lower in the nonsexual group (96.8% unvaccinated vs 87.9% in the sexual group).

南非的 猴痘:病毒如何传播以及如何保护自己 - KAYA 959

Mpox in South Africa: How the virus spreads and how to protect yourself - KAYA 959
新闻简报该文章报告称,南非新增了四例猴痘病例,使五个省份的总病例数增至26例。卫生部确认了这些病例,其中18例位于西开普省,3例位于豪登省,2例位于东开普省,2例位于自由州,1例位于夸祖鲁-纳塔尔省。受影响的人群包括25名男性,年龄在23至50岁之间,中位年龄为37岁。症状包括皮疹、发热、头痛、肌肉疼痛、淋巴结肿大和疲劳。公众被建议避免与疑似病例密切接触,暴露后监测症状,并在有担忧时寻求医疗帮助。17例病例提供了系谱和谱系信息,其中13例与系谱Ib相关,4例与系谱IIb相关。卫生部正在与国家传染病研究所(NICD)及省级当局合作,以加强监测、接触追踪和社区参与,从而控制猴痘的传播。医护人员被敦促保持高度警惕,并遵循感染预防协议。文章强调,不应基于性别、性取向或国籍对个人进行污名化,因为猴痘可能影响任何接触病毒的人。
查看新闻五要素
时间
南非卫生部于2026年10月30日确认新增四例猴痘病例。五个省份的总病例数被报告为26例。

病例记录在南非的五个省份,其中西开普省有18例,豪登省3例,东开普省2例,自由州2例,夸祖鲁-纳塔尔省1例。受影响的人群包括25名男性,年龄在23至50岁之间,中位年龄为37岁。
事件
南非卫生部确认新增四例猴痘病例,使总病例数达到26例。公众被敦促避免与疑似病例密切接触,并在暴露后监测症状。
规模、影响与风险
南非共记录了26例实验室确认的猴痘病例。其中17例提供了系谱和谱系信息,13例与系谱Ib相关,4例与系谱IIb相关。公众被建议如果出现症状应寻求医疗帮助,并避免与他人密切接触以防止进一步传播。
应对、状态与不确定性
南非卫生部正在与国家传染病研究所(NICD)、省级卫生当局及其他利益相关者合作,以加强监测、病例发现、接触追踪、风险沟通和社区参与。医护人员被敦促保持高度警惕,并遵循感染预防和控制协议。

Mpox in South Africa: How the virus spreads and how to protect yourself - KAYA 959

News Brief
The article reports that South Africa has recorded four additional mpox cases, raising the total to 26 across five provinces. The Department of Health confirmed these cases, with 18 in the Western Cape, three in Gauteng, two in the Eastern Cape, two in the Free State, and one in KwaZulu-Natal. The affected population consists of 25 males aged between 23 to 50 years, with a median age of 37. Symptoms include a rash, fever, headache, muscle aches, swollen lymph nodes, and fatigue. The public is advised to avoid close contact with suspected cases, monitor symptoms after exposure, and seek medical attention if concerned. Clade and lineage information is available for 17 cases, with 13 linked to Clade Ib and four to Clade IIb. The Department of Health is collaborating with the National Institute for Communicable Diseases (NICD) and provincial authorities to enhance surveillance, contact tracing, and community engagement to contain the spread of mpox. Healthcare workers are urged to maintain a high index of suspicion and follow infection prevention protocols. The article emphasizes the importance of not stigmatizing individuals based on gender, sexuality, or nationality, as mpox can affect anyone exposed to the virus.

Music streaming service Live radio events Listen to Radio Stream Podcasts Stream Talk Radio Mpox in South Africa: How the virus spreads and how to protect yourself By Mapaballo Borotho Image @WHO South Africa has recorded four additional mpox cases, bringing the total to 26 across five provinces. Symptoms include a rash, fever, headache, muscle aches, swollen lymph nodes and fatigue. The public is urged to avoid close contact with suspected cases, monitor symptoms after exposure and seek medical advice if concerned. The Department of Health has confirmed four additional cases of mpox in South Africa, bringing the total number of laboratory-confirmed cases in the country to 26. Of the 26 cases recorded, 18 were reported in the Western Cape, three in Gauteng, two in the Eastern Cape, two in the Free State and one in KwaZulu-Natal. Health Department spokesperson Foster Mohale said two of the 18 cases in the Western Cape involved foreign nationals who were visiting South Africa, while the remaining 16 were linked to local transmission. “Twenty-five of the 26 cases are male, aged between 23 to 50 years and a median age of 37 years. Clade and lineage information is currently available for 17 cases, of which 13 are associated with the Clade Ib mpox virus and four with Clade IIb,” Mohale said. Members of the public are urged to remain vigilant and seek medical attention if they develop symptoms associated with the illness. What are the symptoms of mpox? The Department of Health has advised the public to look out for the following symptoms: A rash, which may develop into blisters or sores. Fever. Headache. Muscle aches. Swollen lymph nodes. Fatigue. Anyone who suspects they have been in close contact with someone who has mpox is urged to seek medical advice and monitor themselves for symptoms for 21 days. “Should symptoms develop, they should avoid close contact with others and seek medical attention,” Mohale added. How can you protect yourself against mpox? Individuals should avoid close, skin-to-skin contact with anyone who has an mpox-like rash. People should also avoid sharing bedding, towels, clothing and other personal items with someone who has the infection. Healthcare workers are urged to maintain a high index of suspicion when assessing suspected cases and to follow infection prevention and control protocols. “The Department will continue to work closely with the National Institute for Communicable Diseases (NICD), provincial health authorities and other stakeholders to strengthen surveillance, case finding, contact tracing, risk communication and community engagement in order to contain further spread of mpox,” Mohale said. What is mpox? According to the World Health Organisation (WHO), mpox, previously known as monkeypox, is a viral illness caused by the monkeypox virus, which belongs to the Orthopoxvirus genus. The virus has two distinct clades: clade I, which includes subclades Ia and Ib, and clade II, which includes subclades IIa and IIb. The 2022–2023 global mpox outbreak was caused by the clade IIb strain. More recently, an increase in cases in the Democratic Republic of the Congo and other countries linked to clades Ia and Ib has raised international concern. How is mpox transmitted? Mpox can spread through close physical contact with someone who has the infection, including skin-to-skin contact and contact with lesions, bodily fluids or contaminated materials such as clothing and bedding. Transmission can also occur through respiratory particles during prolonged close contact. In healthcare settings, exposure through needlestick injuries is another possible route. The virus can also be transmitted during pregnancy or birth. Infection during pregnancy can pose serious risks to the fetus or newborn, including pregnancy loss, stillbirth and other complications. People who have multiple sexual partners may have a higher risk of exposure, particularly where close physical contact occurs with someone who has the infection. Who is at risk of mpox? According to the WHO, groups that may face a higher risk of exposure include: Healthcare and care workers who may come into contact with the virus. People living in the same household or close community as someone with mpox, including children. People with multiple sexual partners, including men who have sex with men. Sex workers of any gender and their clients. However, mpox can affect anyone who has been exposed to the virus. It is important not to stigmatise people based on their gender, sexuality, occupation or nationality. The WHO says most people with mpox recover within two to four weeks after seeking medical attention. Anyone who suspects they have the infection should seek medical advice promptly, follow healthcare guidance and avoid close contact with others while awaiting assessment. Brazil to send additional mpox vaccine doses to Rio de Janeiro — The Rio Times - UA.NEWS

B routine priorityPublic-health news

Brazil to send additional mpox vaccine doses to Rio de Janeiro — The Rio Times - UA.NEWS

News Brief
Brazil has announced plans to send additional mpox vaccine doses to Rio de Janeiro following an increase in registered cases. The Health Minister, Alexandre Padilha, made the announcement on October 31, 2026, stating that the country is not facing a national outbreak. According to The Rio Times, the city's epidemiological center reported 45 mpox cases in September and 29 in October 2026, totaling 74 cases over the two months—compared to 10 cases during the same period in 2025. Since the start of 2026, Brazil has recorded 665 mpox cases. Padilha noted that the current situation differs significantly from the 2022 outbreak, which saw nearly 11,000 infections. The additional vaccines are aimed at controlling the spread and preparing for major events at the end of the year, without expanding vaccination eligibility or launching a mass campaign. The mpox vaccine is not part of Brazil's national immunization schedule and is recommended for specific high-risk groups.
Original source text (English)
News Health Brazil to send additional mpox vaccine doses to Rio de Janeiro — The Rio Times Fedir Kryshtovskyi 01 November 2026 12:33 Brazil will send additional doses of the mpox vaccine to Rio de Janeiro following an increase in the number of registered cases. Health Minister Alexandre Padilha announced this on October 31, emphasizing that the country is not facing an outbreak at the national level. As The Rio Times reports , the city epidemiological center registered 45 mpox cases in September and another 29 in October 2026. A total of 74 cases were detected over the two months, compared with 10 during the same period in 2025. Preparation for mass events According to Padilha, the additional vaccines are intended to help control the current situation and prepare the city for major events at the end of the year. The minister noted that eligibility for vaccination will not be expanded and that no mass vaccination campaign will be conducted. Additional doses will be directed to areas where the number of cases is increasing. More current news is available on the UA.News Telegram channel Telegram . Since the beginning of 2026, Brazil has recorded 665 mpox cases. Padilha compared the current situation with 2022, when nearly 11,000 people were infected in the country, and stated that the current situation differs significantly from that outbreak. Who can receive the vaccine The mpox vaccine is not included in Brazil's national immunization schedule. It is recommended for laboratory workers who work with related viruses, people living with HIV and weakened immune systems, as well as people who have had direct medium- or high-risk contact with a probable or confirmed case of the disease, following a medical assessment. Mpox spreads primarily through direct contact with skin lesions, bodily fluids, or contaminated objects. Symptoms include fever, body aches, general malaise, and rashes that can develop into blisters. Read us on Download our app

The event is related to Rio de Janeiro, Brazil. The affected population includes individuals who have had direct medium- or high-risk contact with a probable or confirmed mpox case, laboratory workers handling related viruses, and people living with HIV or weakened immune systems.
Event
Brazil will send additional mpox vaccine doses to Rio de Janeiro following an increase in registered cases. This was announced by Health Minister Alexandre Padilha on October 31, 2026.
Scale, impact and risk
A total of 74 mpox cases were detected in September and October 2026, compared to 10 during the same period in 2025. Since the beginning of 2026, Brazil has recorded 665 mpox cases. The current situation is described as significantly different from the 2022 outbreak, which had nearly 11,000 infections.
Response, status and uncertainty
The additional doses are intended to control the current situation and prepare for major events at the end of the year. The minister emphasized that no mass vaccination campaign will be conducted, and eligibility for vaccination will not be expanded.
